# AI-aided content analysis of sustainability communication — course corpus

Each passage starts with a reference ID in square brackets (format aicasc:<module.lesson.item>:<source>:<n>). Cite that ID, the page URL and, for video transcripts, the timestamp.


## Course start

### [aicasc:0:page:001]

source: page (index.qmd) | section: AI-aided content analysis of sustainability communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/index.html

This course introduces AI-aided content analysis of digital sustainability communication. Organizations in every sector now communicate their sustainability work online, through websites, reports, social media and images. The course shows how this growing body of text and visual content can be collected, organized and analyzed systematically, and how computational methods can help answer questions about how organizations present their environmental and social responsibility.

All practical work is done in low-code online notebooks in Google Colab, so you need no software installation and no prior programming experience. With the help of AI-assisted coding, you work step by step through natural language processing of texts and computer vision analysis of images, and learn to summarize, visualize, document and share your results in an open and reproducible way.

The course is designed for students and researchers in the social sciences who want to add computational methods to their toolkit without first becoming programmers. It is equally relevant for communication practitioners in the private and public sectors who want to analyze and evaluate sustainability communication, whether their own organization's or that of others.

## Course start > 0.1 Welcome

### [aicasc:0.1:page:001]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: mooc welcome address | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

Welcome to *AI-Aided Content Analysis of Sustainability Communication*—an online course designed to equip you with cutting-edge tools to explore one of the most pressing challenges of our time: how sustainability is communicated, interpreted, and shaped through digital content.

### [aicasc:0.1:page:002]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: personal presentation | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

My name is Nils Holmberg and i work at the Dept. of Communication at Lund University, where i teach and research on the topics of computational content analysis and cognitive communication effects.

### [aicasc:0.1:page:003]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: paragraph 1 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

Whether you're a Lund University student advancing your coursework in media studies, environmental science, or digital methods, or a researcher or learner joining us from across the globe, this course opens new doors into how we can *see, read, and interpret sustainability communication at scale*. And if you're a curious member of the public—perhaps with an interest in climate issues, artificial intelligence, or media literacy—you’ll find this course a window into how machines can help us make sense of the messages that shape our collective future.

### [aicasc:0.1:page:004]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: paragraph 2 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

From the very start, you’ll get hands-on experience building your own *content database*—learning how to find, collect, and structure sustainability communication from real-world websites. Through accessible, low-code Python notebooks running in Google Colab, you’ll be empowered to run your first analyses with minimal technical barriers. Want to see how organizations talk about climate goals? Curious how different companies portray sustainability through visuals? You'll learn to *read and analyze both text and image content*, using powerful tools from natural language processing and computer vision.

### [aicasc:0.1:page:005]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: paragraph 3 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

For Lund University students, this course offers specific methodological training that can be applied directly to thesis work or project assignments. You’ll gain not only technical fluency but also a critical understanding of how to document your methods and communicate your findings transparently—skills that align with open science values and interdisciplinary research goals.

### [aicasc:0.1:page:006]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: paragraph 5 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

And for members of the general public, this course demystifies AI and data analysis. You won’t just learn *what* these models do—you’ll learn *how to question them*: How does automated analysis shape what we see in sustainability reports? Where do ethical boundaries lie? And how can content analysis be a tool for both transparency and critique?

### [aicasc:0.1:page:007]

source: page (0-1-course-intro.qmd) | section: paragraph 6 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-1-course-intro.html

By the end of this course, you'll be able to extract insights from digital content, summarize and visualize patterns in sustainability communication, and engage with critical discussions about the ethics of AI-assisted research. Whether you're here to meet a curriculum requirement, advance your own research, or explore a personal passion—you're in the right place.

Welcome aboard. Let’s begin.

## Course start > 0.3 Continue

### [aicasc:0.3:page:001]

source: page (0-3-course-outro.qmd) | section: mooc continue address | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-3-course-outro.html

As we wrap up *AI-Aided Content Analysis of Sustainability Communication*, let's reflect on the bridge from decoding digital sustainability messages to exploring their effects on minds and hearts, behaviors and cognitions.

### [aicasc:0.3:page:002]

source: page (0-3-course-outro.qmd) | section: paragraph 1 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-3-course-outro.html

Now, take those content features you've extracted—themes in climate reports, visuals in greenwashing—and use them to test real effects on audiences. With your low-code skills, design experiments to see how messages shape thoughts and feelings in groups or individuals.

### [aicasc:0.3:page:003]

source: page (0-3-course-outro.qmd) | section: paragraph 2 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-3-course-outro.html

Start with surveys: Layer sentiment scores into questions to check attitude shifts—does hopeful framing ease eco-anxiety in young adults? Or try psychophysiological tools like eye-tracking to spot where eyes linger on flood images, or EEG for spikes at guilt trips like "cut your carbon."

### [aicasc:0.3:page:004]

source: page (0-3-course-outro.qmd) | section: paragraph 3 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-3-course-outro.html

For web-based experiments, it's easy and inclusive: A/B test urgency in social media posts with web surveys — does it boost arousal in city vs. countryside folks? Track clicks or bias scores right in browsers, revealing how messages empower or alienate diverse groups.

### [aicasc:0.3:page:005]

source: page (0-3-course-outro.qmd) | section: paragraph 4 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/0-3-course-outro.html

Lund students, this amps up your theses with mixed methods. Researchers, it's a toolkit for stronger papers. Public folks, it shows how to critique comms that truly connect.

You've got the power to test sustainability talk's real touch. Experiment boldly, question deeply—what if our messages heal more than they inform? Stay curious. Until next time.

## Module 1: Understanding sustainability communication content

### [aicasc:1:page:001]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/index.qmd) | section: Module 1: Understanding sustainability communication content | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/index.html

This module introduces sustainability communication as material for content analysis. You learn what characterizes the sustainability content that organizations publish online, and how to collect and organize such content into a small database for analysis in the rest of the course.

### [aicasc:1:page:002]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/index.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/index.html

This lesson introduces sustainability communication content and how it can be studied. The video lecture presents the topic, and in the computer labs you find online sustainability communication from contrasting organizations and set up an initial content database.


## Module 1: Understanding sustainability communication content > Lesson 1.1

### [aicasc:1.1:page:001]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-lesson.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-lesson.html

This lesson introduces sustainability communication content and how it can be studied. The video lecture presents the topic, and in the computer labs you find online sustainability communication from contrasting organizations and set up an initial content database.


## Module 1: Understanding sustainability communication content > Lesson 1.1 > Video lecture 1.1.1: Sustainability communication content

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:001]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Sustainability communication content | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

To understand sustainability communication, we need to define content both formally and functionally. Formally, we can consider sustainability content as any material that aligns with themes of environmental or social responsibility, such as climate action, resource use, or equity. Functionally, sustainability communication serves organizational purposes—such as shaping brand identity, complying with regulations, or engaging stakeholders. These functions affect how audiences perceive credibility and authenticity. Analyzing content through both lenses helps clarify its structure, intent, and potential impact.

---

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:002]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Online content sampling | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

When collecting online sustainability communication for research, sampling methods shape the validity and reliability of findings. Random sampling is considered the gold standard in social science, but it may be difficult to apply in digital contexts. Instead, purposive or strategic sampling can ensure the inclusion of relevant or contrasting cases. Whichever method is used, researchers must assess whether their sample supports generalizable claims. A strong sampling design ensures that observed patterns are not merely artifacts of selection bias.

---

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:003]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Expectations about sustainability communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

Sustainability communication can be studied using qualitative, quantitative, or mixed methods. Qualitative approaches explore the deeper meanings behind content, while quantitative methods enable comparisons and statistical testing. Descriptive studies reveal patterns and practices, while explanatory studies focus on why these patterns occur. For example, a linguistic comparison between high-impact and low-impact organizations might reveal different rhetorical strategies or terminology use. The process begins by formulating clear research questions and operationalizing them into testable hypotheses or coding schemes.

---

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:004]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Find online sustainability communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

The first step in collecting sustainability communication is to select which organizations to study, guided by your research questions. You may focus on a single organization for depth or compare contrasting organizations for breadth. Official websites are a primary source of curated, strategic communication. Within these sites, researchers can collect the URLs of sustainability-related pages, often found under sections like “Sustainability,” “CSR,” or “Environment.” These links will form the basis of your content collection and further analysis.

---

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:005]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Convert multimodal web pages to PDF | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

To capture complex sustainability content online, one effective method is to print web pages to PDF using a browser. This approach preserves multimodal elements such as layout, images, and embedded links, which are essential for analyzing how messages are visually and textually constructed. It's a fast and accessible technique that requires no special tools. However, a limitation is that videos or dynamic elements are not captured, making this best suited for static page content.

---

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:006]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Setting up an initial content database | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

After collecting the content, it’s important to store it in a way that supports reproducibility and easy access. Saving files locally is straightforward and works offline, but can be harder to share or back up. Using cloud platforms like Google Drive or GitHub offers better collaboration, version control, and accessibility. Regardless of the method, the goal is to make communication content systematically available for analysis, and to ensure it can be traced back to its source.

---

### [aicasc:1.1.1:page:007]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Sustainability communication content > lecture text > Organize communication content in folders | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

Organizing files into folders by organization or theme helps compare sustainability messaging across cases. Consistent file path naming is important for automating the analysis and maintaining structure. If content is saved as rendered PDFs, it may need to be converted into plain text later for textual analysis or coding. A clear folder structure simplifies navigation, supports reproducibility, and ensures that data management doesn't become a barrier to insight.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:001]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Sustainability communication content | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

When we talk about sustainability communication, think of it in two complementary ways: formally, as messages that explicitly reference environmental or social responsibility, and functionally, as content that advances branding, compliance, and stakeholder engagement. Both dimensions shape how audiences judge credibility and authenticity. By reading the form and the function together, we can see intent, the structural choices behind a message, and its likely impact on different audiences.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:002]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Online content sampling | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

How you sample online content will determine what you can claim from your study. Random sampling is the gold standard but tricky on the web, so we often use purposive sampling to capture the most relevant or contrasting cases, and strategic sampling to balance focus with generalizability. Whatever you choose, design your sampling to minimize bias so your findings are reliable and valid.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:003]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Expectations about sustainability communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

Decide early whether your study is qualitative, quantitative, or mixed-methods, because that choice guides the questions you ask and the evidence you need. Descriptive analyses map patterns; explanatory analyses test why those patterns occur. Linguistic comparisons—tone, terminology, rhetorical strategies—help turn observations into arguments, but everything starts with well-formed questions and hypotheses and a transparent method that others can interpret and replicate.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:004]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Find online sustainability communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

Begin by selecting organizations that fit your research goals—one for depth or several for contrast—and treat official websites as curated sources of messaging. Identify sustainability-related sections and URLs (e.g., “CSR,” “Sustainability,” “Impact”) and extract them systematically. The aim is to build a targeted dataset that directly reflects how organizations present their sustainability commitments.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:005]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Convert multimodal web pages to PDF | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

A quick way to capture web content is to print pages to PDF, preserving the images, layout, and other static design elements that shape meaning. This gives you a portable record of multimodal communication without writing code, but remember the limitation: videos and interactive features won’t be captured, so note those gaps in your method.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:006]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Setting up an initial content database | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

Store everything in accessible formats from day one. Local folders are simple, but cloud options like Google Drive improve collaboration and backup, while GitHub adds version control and traceability. A clean, documented archive—what was collected, when, and from where—sets you up for consistent, long-term analysis and easier verification.

### [aicasc:1.1.1:slides:007]

source: slides (tex/1-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Organize communication content in folders | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-1-video-lecture.html

Use a clear folder hierarchy—by organization, year, or campaign—to support comparisons and batch processing. Normalize filenames and paths so scripts can find and process files reliably, and plan for OCR or text extraction from PDFs if you’ll analyze language features. Good organization isn’t busywork; it’s the backbone of transparency and reproducibility.

## Module 1: Understanding sustainability communication content > Lesson 1.1 > Computer lab 1.1.2: Find online sustainability communication

### [aicasc:1.1.2:page:001]

source: page (module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-2-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 1.1: Find online sustainability communication > lab text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module01-understanding-sustainability-communication-content-10pct/1-1-2-computer-lab.html

In this lab you will find online sustainability communication to use as material for content analysis. Starting from an ordinary web search, you locate the websites of organizations that publish sustainability content and identify the pages where they present their sustainability work, such as dedicated sustainability sections, reports and news pages.

To make later comparisons meaningful, the lab uses contrasting organizations, for example an energy company and an environmental NGO, that communicate about the same topic from different positions. You then capture the selected web pages as PDF files, which preserves both their text and images as a stable record that can be analyzed in the coming modules.

In this lab you will set up an initial content database for your project. The collected PDF files are organized in a single project directory with one subdirectory per organization, so that the folder structure itself records which organization each document comes from. This simple convention is what later code uses to group and compare results.

You then make the content available online, either through Google Drive or a GitHub repository, and learn how to bring it into a Google Colab session. The notebook shows how to compress the project directory into a zip file, upload it to the Colab file area, and unzip it so that the documents are ready for text and image analysis.

## Module 2: Introduction to low-code Python programming > Lesson 2.1 > Video lecture 2.1.1: AI-aided and low-code programming

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:001]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **Open Science Methods** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Open science promotes research practices that are transparent, reproducible, and openly accessible. It encourages international collaboration through the open sharing of data, code, and methodologies, making research more verifiable and impactful. By relying on open-source tools and platforms, it breaks down barriers between disciplines and enhances the credibility and integrity of scientific output. Ultimately, open science fosters innovation by enabling broader participation in the research process.

---

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:002]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **Python Environments: Local, Cloud, Notebooks** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Python can be run in different environments depending on the user’s needs. Local environments require installing Python and managing dependencies on your own machine, which offers control but can be complex. Cloud environments like Google Colab remove the need for setup, providing instant access to computational resources. Jupyter notebooks, used both locally and in the cloud, allow for interactive coding with integrated documentation. This flexibility makes notebooks especially useful for iterative analysis and educational purposes.

---

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:003]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **The Concept of Jupyter Notebooks** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Jupyter Notebooks offer a powerful way to write and run code in small, interactive steps. Each notebook combines live code, explanations, and visualizations in a single document, making it ideal for experimentation, learning, and communication of results. They support a workflow where code can be modified and tested incrementally, which is especially helpful in debugging and teaching. Compared to traditional script-based environments, notebooks provide a more transparent and readable approach to programming.

---

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:004]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **Google Colab Notebook Interface** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Google Colab is a free, cloud-based platform that hosts Jupyter notebooks and provides access to GPUs and other computing resources. It features an interface where users can write and execute code in cells while documenting their process in markdown. The notebook automatically saves to Google Drive, supporting easy sharing and collaboration. This makes it a perfect choice for beginners or teams who want to start coding without the hassle of installing anything locally.

---

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:005]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **What Is Programming and Python Programming** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Programming is the process of writing instructions that computers can follow to perform tasks, from calculations to automation. Python, known for its readable syntax and versatility, is one of the most popular languages for both beginners and professionals. It’s widely used in fields such as web development, data science, machine learning, and more. With its vast ecosystem of libraries, Python simplifies many tasks, making it accessible for quick prototyping and robust application development alike.

---

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:006]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **Python Use Cases: Low vs. High-Code** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Python supports both low-code and high-code workflows depending on user expertise and project complexity. Low-code approaches are useful for quick data analysis and visualization, especially in fields like social science where rapid insight is key. In contrast, high-code solutions enable more advanced customization, often used in computer science and AI development. The strength of Python lies in its ability to serve both ends of the spectrum efficiently.

---

### [aicasc:2.1.1:page:007]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: AI-aided and low-code programming > lecture text > **Basic Python Syntax: Variables, Data Objects, Loops** | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Understanding Python's basic syntax is the first step in learning to program effectively. Variables are used to store information, and Python’s built-in data types like lists, dictionaries, and tuples help organize it. Loops enable automation by allowing code to repeat across items or conditions. Python’s syntax is intentionally clean and intuitive, which reduces errors and makes code easier to read. These fundamentals are the building blocks for writing useful and scalable programs.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:001]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Open Science Methods | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Open science is about doing research in a way that others can understand, trust, and build on. We make our work reproducible and transparent, we collaborate across institutions and countries, and we share our data, code, and methods openly. Using open-source tools and platforms makes this practical, lowers barriers to entry, and accelerates feedback. In short, openness strengthens scientific integrity and speeds up innovation.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:002]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Python Environments: Local, Cloud, Notebooks | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

You can run Python locally on your computer, in the cloud, or inside interactive notebooks. Local setups give you full control but require installation and configuration; cloud options like Google Colab remove setup hurdles and offer scalable resources. Notebooks combine runnable code with narrative text, so you can explain, execute, and document in one place—perfect for live demos, iterative work, and reproducible analyses.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:003]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: The Concept of Jupyter Notebooks | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Jupyter lets you execute code step by step, mix it with explanatory text, and embed visualizations—all in a single document. This supports rapid experimentation, easy debugging, and thorough documentation, which is ideal for both teaching and research. Compared to traditional scripts, notebooks keep your reasoning, results, and code together, making your workflow clearer and easier to share.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:004]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Google Colab Notebook Interface | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Colab is a cloud-hosted Jupyter environment with optional GPU access and zero local setup. You work in cells—some for code, some for text—while your progress auto-saves and can sync with Google Drive. It’s built for collaboration and quick sharing, so teams can comment, copy, and rerun analyses easily. For fast prototyping and student projects, it’s a great default.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:005]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: What Is Programming and Python Programming | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Programming is simply writing clear instructions for a computer to follow. Python is a readable, high-level language that’s versatile enough for web apps, data science, and automation. Its rich ecosystem of libraries turns complex tasks into a few lines of code, which makes it welcoming for beginners and powerful for professionals. That combination is why we use it here.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:006]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Python Use Cases: Low vs. High-Code | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

Low-code Python emphasizes speed and clarity—loading data, cleaning it, and visualizing results with minimal boilerplate—ideal for social science analysis. High-code projects dive deeper into custom algorithms, performance tuning, and complex systems—more common in computer science applications. Python spans both worlds, so you can start simple and scale your skills as your projects grow.

### [aicasc:2.1.1:slides:007]

source: slides (tex/2-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Basic Python Syntax: Variables, Data Objects, Loops | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-1-video-lecture.html

At the core, variables hold values and data types define what those values are. You’ll organize information with lists, dictionaries, and tuples, then use loops to repeat actions over items—great for automating routine steps. Python’s clean syntax keeps this readable, which is why mastering these basics unlocks everything else you’ll do in analysis and automation.

## Module 2: Introduction to low-code Python programming > Lesson 2.1 > Computer lab 2.1.2: Google Colab notebook user interface

### [aicasc:2.1.2:page:001]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-2-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: Google Colab notebook user interface > lab text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-2-computer-lab.html

This lab introduces the Google Colab notebook interface, which is used for all computer labs in the course. You practice creating, deleting, moving and running code cells and text cells, and see how text cells written in Markdown can document your analysis alongside the code.

You also explore the left-hand menu, with its table of contents, find-and-replace, secrets, files and variables panels, and the top menu, where you open and save notebooks, change notebook settings and restart the session. Getting comfortable with these tools makes the later analysis labs much easier to follow.

### [aicasc:2.1.2:transcript:001]

And we are going to talk about the different menus and the different file areas inside Google Colab.

## Module 2: Introduction to low-code Python programming > Lesson 2.1 > Computer lab 2.1.3: Trying out some low-code Python

### [aicasc:2.1.3:page:001]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-3-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.1: Trying out some low-code Python > lab text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-3-computer-lab.html

In this lab you try out low-code Python programming with the help of Colab's built-in AI assistant. Instead of writing code from scratch, you describe what you want in a natural-language prompt, for example a function that checks whether a word is a palindrome, and let the assistant generate the code.

You then run and test the generated function, and practice AI-assisted debugging by asking the assistant to explain and fix a short piece of faulty code. The lab shows how prompting, reading and testing code go together, a workflow you will keep using throughout the course.

### [aicasc:2.1.3:notebook:002]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: AI-assisted code functions | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-1-3-computer-lab.html

```python
# prompt: generate a python function to check if a given string is a palindrome, and test the function

def is_palindrome(s):
  """Checks if a string is a palindrome."""
  s = s.lower().replace(" ", "")  # Convert to lowercase and remove spaces
  return s == s[::-1]

# Test the function
print(is_palindrome("madam"))
print(is_palindrome("hello"))
print(is_palindrome("A man a plan a canal Panama"))
print(is_palindrome("racecar"))
print(is_palindrome("google"))
print(is_palindrome("level"))
```

## Module 2: Introduction to low-code Python programming > Lesson 2.2

### [aicasc:2.2:page:001]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-lesson.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-lesson.html

This lesson applies low-code Python to data analysis. The video lecture shows how Python supports computational data analysis, and in the computer labs you use AI-assisted coding to create, aggregate and save tables, and to visualize them as graphs.


## Module 2: Introduction to low-code Python programming > Lesson 2.2 > Video lecture 2.2.1: Python and AI-aided data analysis

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:001]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > Python for Social Science Data Analysis | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

In the social sciences, data analysis takes many forms—including descriptive statistics, inferential testing, and exploratory modeling. Python provides a flexible and scalable environment for performing all of these, with a growing ecosystem tailored to academic and applied research. One emerging area is computational content analysis, where researchers apply Python tools to analyze text and visual content systematically. Libraries such as `pandas`, `statsmodels`, `matplotlib`, and `scikit-learn` enable everything from data wrangling to advanced modeling, making Python an essential tool for quantitative social scientists.

---

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:002]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > AI-Aided Quantitative Analysis with Python | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

Artificial intelligence—especially large language models—now supports researchers in performing quantitative analysis more efficiently. These models can generate Python code on demand for statistical tasks, allowing users to interact with their data through natural language. This reduces the technical barrier for non-programmers while enabling rapid hypothesis testing, data cleaning, and model generation. The balance between manual coding and AI-assisted scripting gives researchers more control and efficiency, particularly when iterating through complex analytical workflows.

---

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:003]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > AI-Aided Computational Content Analysis | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

AI is also transforming how we approach content analysis. Large language models can assist in extracting meaning from text through classification, summarization, or topic modeling. Meanwhile, vision models like CLIP or ViT enable analysis of image and video content. Together, these tools support multimodal analysis, where textual and visual data are examined in tandem. Researchers can automate tasks such as entity recognition and theme detection, significantly speeding up the analysis of large, complex corpora while still allowing for nuanced interpretation.

---

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:004]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > Low-Code Data Analysis with Tables | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

Python makes it easy to read and manipulate tabular data, especially using the `pandas` library. With AI-assisted tools, users can perform complex filtering, grouping, and aggregation with simple prompts rather than verbose code. This low-code approach is ideal for exploratory data analysis, especially in non-technical fields. Tools like `pandasgui`, `datatable`, or conversational agents like ChatGPT can generate, execute, and explain table operations in real-time. This democratizes access to data insights and facilitates reproducibility.

---

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:005]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > Data Transformations with AI Assistance | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

Transforming data—through selection, filtering, and aggregation—is foundational to any analysis workflow. In Python, these operations are traditionally handled with `pandas`, but AI tools can now suggest or execute them via natural language instructions. This makes it easier for users to identify the variables they need, set conditions for filtering, and compute summaries like group means or totals. By comparing AI-generated queries to traditional code, learners gain a deeper understanding of the logic behind data manipulation.

---

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:006]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > Low-Code Data Analysis with Graphs | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

Visualization is a key part of understanding and communicating data. Python supports a wide range of basic plotting options, including bar charts, line graphs, and histograms. With AI assistance, users can describe the kind of graph they want and receive ready-to-run code. This enables quick generation of univariate and bivariate plots that explore single variables or pairwise relationships. Libraries such as `matplotlib`, `seaborn`, and `plotnine` provide powerful tools for crafting clear, insightful visualizations with minimal setup.

---

### [aicasc:2.2.1:page:007]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Python and AI-aided data analysis > lecture text > Multivariate and Interactive Graphs | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

When analysis involves more than two variables, visualization becomes more complex—and more powerful. Python offers tools to create multivariate plots like scatter matrices, heatmaps, and bubble charts. For dynamic exploration, libraries like `plotly`, `altair`, and `holoviews` enable interactive features such as zooming, filtering, and tooltip displays. AI support can refine these visualizations by adjusting parameters or recommending plot types based on data structure. This helps users engage deeply with high-dimensional data and uncover hidden patterns.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:001]

source: slides (tex/2-2-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Python for Social Science Data Analysis | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

In this module, we map out descriptive, inferential, and exploratory analysis with a social-science lens, then show how computational content analysis spans text and images. Python shines here because it’s flexible and scalable—from quick prototypes to full studies—while libraries like `pandas`, `statsmodels`, `matplotlib`, and `scikit-learn` let you clean data, estimate models, and visualize results with just a few lines. The goal is to make rigorous quantitative methods feel approachable and repeatable.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:002]

source: slides (tex/2-2-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: AI-Aided Quantitative Analysis with Python | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

We’ll use large language models to draft statistical code, query datasets in natural language, and automate routine steps like hypothesis tests and model fitting. This lowers the entry barrier for non-programmers while keeping you in the driver’s seat, so you can balance fast AI assistance with manual checks and methodological control.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:003]

source: slides (tex/2-2-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: AI-Aided Computational Content Analysis | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

Here we put AI to work on text and visuals: LLMs for classification, summarization, and topic modeling, and image models like CLIP and ViT for recognizing themes in visual content. By combining text and image features, automating entity detection, and scaling to large corpora, you get a richer view of sustainability communication without losing analytical rigor.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:004]

source: slides (tex/2-2-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Low-Code Data Analysis with Tables | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

We’ll load datasets into `pandas` DataFrames, then filter, group, and summarize with minimal code—often generated or suggested by AI. You’ll see how low-code workflows (with tools like `pandasgui`, `datatable`, or ChatGPT + CSV) compare to higher-code scripts, and how AI can both produce the table and help interpret what it shows.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:005]

source: slides (tex/2-2-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Data Transformations with AI Assistance | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

You’ll practice selecting variables, filtering observations by time or value ranges, and aggregating to group-level summaries. We’ll contrast classic `pandas` syntax with AI-assisted queries, using the back-and-forth with the model to clarify logic, spot mistakes, and learn better transformation patterns as you go.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:006]

source: slides (tex/2-2-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Low-Code Data Analysis with Graphs | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-1-video-lecture.html

We’ll quickly build core plots—bars, lines, histograms—and talk about when to use univariate versus bivariate views. AI can propose suitable chart types and draft `matplotlib`, `seaborn`, or `plotnine` code, so you can visualize trends and relationships with almost no setup while still keeping outputs clear and publication-ready.

### [aicasc:2.2.1:slides:007]

To explore richer patterns, we’ll use scatter matrices, heatmaps, and bubble charts, then add interactivity with `plotly`, `altair`, or `holoviews`. AI can help refine variable choices, axes, and layout, letting you create dynamic views that invite exploration and make complex relationships in large datasets easier to understand.

## Module 2: Introduction to low-code Python programming > Lesson 2.2 > Computer lab 2.2.2: Low-code data analysis, tables

### [aicasc:2.2.2:page:001]

source: page (module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-2-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 2.2: Low-code data analysis, tables > lab text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-2-computer-lab.html

This lab applies AI-assisted coding to tabular data analysis with the pandas library. Using short prompts, you generate a small example dataframe with an integer identifier, a random numeric variable and a categorical variable, and inspect its structure.

You then aggregate the data, calculating the mean of the numeric variable for each category, and save both the original and the aggregated tables as tab-separated (TSV) files. Loading the files back into dataframes shows how intermediate results can be stored and reused between notebook sessions.

### [aicasc:2.2.2:notebook:002]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: Use AI-assisted dataframes | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-2-computer-lab.html

```python
# prompt: Generate a pandas dataset with three variables, one unique integer, one random float, one categorical

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

# Generate unique integers
unique_integers = np.arange(100)

# Generate random floats
random_floats = np.random.rand(100)

# Generate categorical data
categories = ['A', 'B', 'C']
categorical_data = np.random.choice(categories, 100)

# Create a DataFrame
df = pd.DataFrame({
    'unique_integer': unique_integers,
    'random_float': random_floats,
    'category': categorical_data
})

print(df.head())
```

### [aicasc:2.2.2:notebook:003]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: AI-assisted data aggregation | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-2-computer-lab.html

```python
# Use dataframe "df" and aggregate mean of column "random_float" by column "category"
```

```python
# prompt: Use dataframe "df" and aggregate mean of column "random_float" by column "category"

# Group by 'category' and calculate the mean of 'random_float'
mean_by_category = df.groupby('category')['random_float'].mean()

mean_by_category
```

### [aicasc:2.2.2:notebook:004]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: Save and load dataframes | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-2-computer-lab.html

```python
# Save dataframes "df" and "mean_by_category" as tsv files, then load tsv file to existing dataframe
```

```python
# prompt: Save dataframes "df" and "mean_by_category" as tsv files, then load tsv file to existing dataframe

import pandas as pd
df.to_csv('df.tsv', sep='\t', index=False)
mean_by_category.to_csv('mean_by_category.tsv', sep='\t')

# Load tsv file to existing dataframe df
df = pd.read_csv('df.tsv', sep='\t')
```

In this lab you turn tables into graphs using AI-assisted data visualization. After loading the aggregated data saved in the previous lab, you prompt the assistant to create a bar plot of the category means with matplotlib, and adjust the appearance of the plot, such as axis limits and labels.

You go on to create line graphs with seaborn, and explore the seaborn example gallery to try more advanced plot types. The aim is to see how quickly a visualization can be produced from a clear prompt, and how to refine it until it communicates the result well.

### [aicasc:2.2.3:notebook:002]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: Use AI-assisted data visualization | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-3-computer-lab.html

```python
import pandas as pd

# Load tsv file to existing dataframe df
mean_by_category = pd.read_csv('mean_by_category.tsv', sep='\t')

mean_by_category.head()
```

```python
# prompt: Use dataframe "mean_by_category" and generate a bar plot of "random_float" by "category"

import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Customize appearance
ymin = 0      # Set your desired y-axis min
ymax = 1      # Set your desired y-axis max
bar_color = 'green'  # Custom bar color

# Reset index so 'category' becomes a column
df_plot = mean_by_category.reset_index()

# Create bar plot
plt.figure(figsize=(8, 5))
plt.bar(df_plot['category'], df_plot['random_float'], color=bar_color)

# Format axes
plt.title('Mean of Random Float by Category')
plt.xlabel('Category')
plt.ylabel('Mean Random Float')
plt.ylim(ymin, ymax)
plt.xticks(rotation=0, ha='right')

plt.tight_layout()

# Save the plot
output_file = 'mean_by_category_plot.png'  # Set your desired filename
plt.savefig(output_file, dpi=300)

plt.show()
```

### [aicasc:2.2.3:notebook:003]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: AI-assisted line graphs | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-3-computer-lab.html

# prompt: i have dataframe below, please create a seaborn line graph of first two columns, first column on x axis and second on y axis

#     'random_float': random_floats,
#     'category': categorical_data

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import seaborn as sns

plt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))
sns.lineplot(x='unique_integer', y='random_float', data=df)
plt.title('Random Float vs. Unique Integer')
plt.xlabel('Unique Integer')
plt.ylabel('Random Float')
plt.show()

### [aicasc:2.2.3:notebook:004]

source: notebook (jnb/aicasc/module02_introduction_to_low_code_python_programming_20pct.ipynb) | section: More AI-assisted graphs | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module02-introduction-to-low-code-python-programming-20pct/2-2-3-computer-lab.html

```python
# https://seaborn.pydata.org/examples/index.html

import seaborn as sns
sns.set_theme(style="darkgrid")

# Load an example dataset with long-form data
fmri = sns.load_dataset("fmri")

# Plot the responses for different events and regions
sns.lineplot(x="timepoint", y="signal",
             hue="region", style="event",
             data=fmri)
```


## Module 3: Analyzing text content with natural language processing

### [aicasc:3:page:001]

This module applies natural language processing (NLP) to the texts in your sustainability communication database. You read texts into dataframes, describe them, annotate them with tokens, parts of speech and named entities, and summarize and visualize the results.

### [aicasc:3:page:002]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/index.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1 | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/index.html

This lesson introduces natural language processing (NLP) in social science. The video lecture presents the field, and in the computer labs you convert PDF documents to plain text, read the texts into a dataframe and compute descriptive text statistics.

This lesson covers inferential text analysis. The video lecture explains part-of-speech tagging and named entity recognition, and in the computer labs you split texts into sentences and tokens with spaCy and annotate each token with its part of speech and entity type.

## Module 3: Analyzing text content with natural language processing > Lesson 3.1 > Video lecture 3.1.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:001]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > Functions of Texts in Sustainability Communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Texts in sustainability communication serve varied purposes, including raising awareness, educating audiences, advocating for change, and influencing policies. Informational texts like reports and white papers provide detailed data and analyses, while persuasive content such as blogs and social media posts aim to inspire action. Visual- and data-supported texts often enhance engagement by presenting complex ideas in accessible formats. Understanding the intended function is critical for effective communication and targeted NLP applications.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:002]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > NLP Areas and Challenges of Unstructured Text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Natural Language Processing (NLP) covers diverse areas, from sentiment analysis to machine translation and summarization. Working with unstructured text presents challenges, such as handling ambiguous language, idiomatic expressions, and domain-specific jargon. Additionally, processing noisy data from social media or OCR errors in scanned documents often complicates the analysis. Effective NLP requires robust preprocessing pipelines and domain-specific adjustments to achieve meaningful results.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:003]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > Text Analysis Basics: Units, Tokens, and N-grams | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

In NLP, text analysis begins with defining the units of analysis—such as sentences, words, or characters. Tokens, the smallest logical units of text, are derived from splitting strings into meaningful components. N-grams, sequences of n tokens, capture contextual relationships, with bigrams and trigrams being particularly useful for understanding phrases. Mastering these foundational concepts is essential for advanced text processing.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:004]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > Formats and Conversion to Plain Text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Text is often embedded in formats like PDFs, HTML, or Markdown, each presenting unique challenges for extraction. PDFs may include layout artifacts, while HTML contains tags that must be parsed. Converting these formats into clean plain text ensures compatibility with NLP tools. Tools like Tika, Beautiful Soup, and Markdown parsers simplify this conversion process, paving the way for structured analysis.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:005]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > Text Content Features: Readability, POS, and NER | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Key features of text content include readability indices like the Flesch Reading Ease, which assess complexity, and linguistic features such as Part-of-Speech (POS) tagging, which categorizes words based on their grammatical role. Named Entity Recognition (NER) identifies and classifies proper nouns, dates, or other specific entities. These features offer insights into the style, structure, and meaning of the text, aiding both analysis and decision-making.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:006]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > Reading Text into Dataframes and Preprocessing | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Textual data is often ingested into dataframes for analysis, enabling structured workflows. This process involves normalization tasks, such as lowercasing and removing punctuation, followed by tokenization to split the text into analyzable units. Pandas and NLTK are popular tools for managing these steps, enabling efficient preparation of text for downstream NLP tasks.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:page:007]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Natural language processing (NLP) in social science > lecture text > Manifest Text Content and Frequency Analysis | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Manifest content refers to the explicit, observable elements of a text, such as word counts, sentence lengths, and overall structure. Techniques like word frequency analysis reveal dominant themes and help identify key terms. Sentence and word counts provide quantitative measures of text characteristics, while visualization tools like word clouds offer intuitive insights into content prominence.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:001]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Functions of Texts in Sustainability Communication | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Texts play different roles: informational pieces provide data-driven facts, persuasive messages aim to motivate action, and narratives build emotional connection to sustainability themes. When paired with visuals—figures, images, or icons—texts become more accessible and engaging. The key is to match text type to audience and goal so each message lands with clarity and purpose.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:002]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: NLP and Challenges of Unstructured Text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

NLP helps with tasks like sentiment analysis, translation, and summarization, but real-world text is messy: it’s ambiguous, full of domain jargon and colloquialisms, and often noisy—especially on social media. Robust preprocessing pipelines and well-chosen algorithms are essential to clean, normalize, and model this unstructured input so findings remain valid and reproducible.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:003]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Basic Concepts: Units, Tokens, and N-grams | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

We analyze text at multiple levels—characters, words, and sentences—with tokens as the basic units. N-grams extend this by capturing sequences of tokens (like bigrams and trigrams), revealing common phrases and local context. These building blocks underpin downstream tasks, from classification and topic modeling to sentiment and sequence labeling.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:004]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Formats and Conversion to Plain Text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Text arrives as PDFs, HTML, Markdown, and more, each with quirks. PDFs can carry layout artifacts; HTML needs parsing to strip tags and isolate meaningful content. Tools like Beautiful Soup and PDFMiner help convert diverse sources into clean, plain text—creating a consistent starting point for any NLP workflow.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:005]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Text Features: Readability, POS, and NER | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Readability indices gauge how complex a text is, POS tagging labels words by grammatical role, and NER pulls out entities such as people, organizations, dates, and places. Together, these features illuminate style, structure, and context, providing interpretable signals that improve modeling and deepen thematic understanding.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:006]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Reading Text into Dataframes and Preprocessing | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

We load documents into dataframes to align text with metadata and visualizations, then normalize by lowercasing and removing punctuation. Tokenization splits text into analyzable units, often with `pandas` orchestrating data handling and NLTK (or similar) handling linguistic steps. Clean, tokenized data is the prerequisite for almost every NLP task.

### [aicasc:3.1.1:slides:007]

source: slides (tex/3-1-1-video-lecture-slides.txt) | section: Manifest Text Content and Frequency Analysis | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-1-video-lecture.html

Manifest content focuses on what’s explicitly present: counts of sentences and words, and frequency of terms. Simple frequency tables and plots surface dominant themes quickly, while visualizations—like word clouds—offer fast intuition. These descriptive metrics are the first pass in exploratory text analysis and set up deeper modeling that follows.

## Module 3: Analyzing text content with natural language processing > Lesson 3.1 > Computer lab 3.1.2: Reading text into dataframes

### [aicasc:3.1.2:page:001]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-2-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Reading text into dataframes > lab text > Convert PDF to plain text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-2-computer-lab.html

```python
!pip install -q pdfminer.six
```

```python
import os
from pdfminer.high_level import extract_text

# Directories containing the PDFs
directories = ['organization1', 'organization2']
directories = ['/content/osm-cca-nlp/res/pdf/preem', '/content/osm-cca-nlp/res/pdf/vattenfall']

for directory in directories:
    for root, dirs, files in os.walk(directory):
        for file in files:
            if file.lower().endswith('.pdf'):
                pdf_path = os.path.join(root, file)
                text_path = os.path.splitext(pdf_path)[0] + '.txt'

                try:
                    text = extract_text(pdf_path)
                    with open(text_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
                        f.write(text)
                    print(f"Converted {pdf_path} to {text_path}")
                except Exception as e:
                    print(f"Failed to convert {pdf_path}: {e}")
```

**Importing Necessary Libraries**

The code begins by importing essential modules. It imports `os` for interacting with the operating system's file system and `extract_text` from `pdfminer.high_level` for extracting text content from PDF files.

---

**Defining the Directories Containing PDFs**

Two lists named `directories` are defined. The first is a placeholder with `['organization1', 'organization2']`, and the second specifies the actual paths to the directories containing the PDF files:
- `/content/osm-cca-nlp/res/pdf/preem`
- `/content/osm-cca-nlp/res/pdf/vattenfall`

---

**Iterating Over Each Directory**

The code uses a `for` loop to iterate through each directory specified in the `directories` list. This allows the program to process multiple directories sequentially.

---

**Walking Through Directory Trees**

Within each directory, the `os.walk(directory)` function traverses the directory tree. It yields a tuple containing the `root` path, a list of `dirs` (subdirectories), and a list of `files` in each directory.

---

**Identifying PDF Files**

For every file in the `files` list, the code checks if the file name ends with `.pdf` (case-insensitive) using `file.lower().endswith('.pdf')`. This ensures that only PDF files are processed.

---

**Constructing File Paths**

The full path to the PDF file is constructed using `os.path.join(root, file)`. The corresponding text file path is created by replacing the `.pdf` extension with `.txt` using `os.path.splitext(pdf_path)[0] + '.txt'`.

---

**Extracting Text from PDFs**

A `try` block is initiated to attempt text extraction. The `extract_text(pdf_path)` function reads the content of the PDF file and stores it in the variable `text`.

---

**Writing Extracted Text to Files**

If text extraction is successful, the code opens a new text file at `text_path` in write mode with UTF-8 encoding. It writes the extracted text into this file and then closes it, ensuring the text is saved next to the original PDF.

---

**Logging Successful Conversions**

After successfully writing the text file, the code prints a message indicating the PDF file has been converted, using:

```python
print(f"Converted {pdf_path} to {text_path}")
```

---

**Handling Exceptions**

### [aicasc:3.1.2:page:002]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-2-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Reading text into dataframes > lab text > Convert PDF to plain text | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-2-computer-lab.html

An `except` block catches any exceptions that occur during the extraction or writing process. If an error occurs, it prints a failure message with the path of the PDF file and the exception details:

```python
print(f"Failed to convert {pdf_path}: {e}")
```

---

### [aicasc:3.1.2:page:003]

source: page (module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-2-computer-lab.qmd) | section: Lesson 3.1: Reading text into dataframes > lab text > Read plain text to Pandas Dataframe | page: https://cca-cce.github.io/osm-cca/web/ai-aided-content-analysis-of-sustainability-communication/module03-analyzing-text-content-natural-language-processing-30pct/3-1-2-computer-lab.html

```python
import os
import pandas as pd
import re
import string

# Directories containing the text files
directories = ['organization1', 'organization2']
directories = ['/content/osm-cca-nlp/res/pdf/preem', '/content/osm-cca-nlp/res/pdf/vattenfall']

data = []
text_index = 1

# Allowed characters: alphabetic, punctuation, and whitespace
allowed_chars = set(string.ascii_letters + string.punctuation + string.whitespace)

for directory in directories:
    for root, dirs, files in os.walk(directory):
        for file in files:
            if file.lower().endswith('.txt'):
                file_path = os.path.join(root, file)
                folder_name = os.path.basename(root)

                with open(file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
                    raw_text = f.read()

                # Keep only allowed characters
                clean_text = ''.join(c for c in raw_text if c in allowed_chars)

                # Replace sequences of whitespace with a single space
                clean_text = re.sub(r'\s+', ' ', clean_text)

                # Trim leading and trailing whitespace
                clean_text = clean_text.strip()

                data.append({
                    'text_index': text_index,
                    'file_path': file_path,
                    'folder_name': folder_name,
                    'raw_text': raw_text,
                    'clean_text': clean_text
                })

                text_index += 1

# Create DataFrame
df_texts = pd.DataFrame(data, columns=['text_index', 'file_path', 'folder_name', 'raw_text', 'clean_text'])

# Save DataFrame to TSV file
df_texts.to_csv('df_texts.tsv', sep='\t', index=False)
```
